# Fact-check ledger

Cameron's manual-review queue from the editorial review
(sources/plan-review.md). One row per flag; written by the fact-check and
external-verification passes and by resolve-cites. **A row is a question,
not a verdict** — single-source ≠ wrong; nothing is deleted on the strength
of a flag. Categories: single-source | contradicted-by-source |
contradicted-internal | external-mismatch | cite-unresolved | unverifiable.
The external pass appends ` — verified-current <date> (<source>)` or
` — external-mismatch: …` to the detail cell of rows tagged `ext-verify`.

**Three sections, and only the first is a queue** (Cameron, 2026-08-26 —
"rest you can handle on your own"). The table between the markers holds the
rows that still need something from outside the corpus: each is tagged
`ext-verify` and is an input to `verify-external.yml`, the only workflow with
web access. Below the markers, **Standing** holds rows whose disposition the
KB's own rules already decide — the claim stands under flag, and the note
still carries that flag — and **Resolved** holds rows that are closed, with
how. Rows below the markers are invisible to the dashboard and to the
external verifier by design; they are the record, not the work.

| species/barracuda.md | a barracuda left in the sun all day goes to mush, the meat falling out of the fish when it is cut | contradicted-by-source | `5to3Q5P7w90` | the mush and the meat falling out when cut are said of a **dorado** brought to work in a gunny sack; barracuda enters only as the audience simile — "you guys remember the barracuda days, like a barracuda's been sitting in the sun all day". The corpus therefore contains no description of barracuda spoilage at all, yet the note's whole Landing & handling doctrine is built on it. The evidence file already records the dorado framing correctly, so the hardening happened in the note — **ext-verify**: whether Pacific barracuda flesh is unusually prone to rapid post-mortem softening relative to other SoCal species, and what handling window follows |
| species/bluefin-tuna-trolling.md | speed-trolling a Mad Mac in the 13–15 kt band, quoted as the tool "for locating fish and finding bites when you don't have the conditions to put that kite up" | contradicted-by-source | `z1CmWHqe9uk` | the transcript attaches 13–15 kt to two OTHER lures and hedges it — the Rapala Sarda ("make a track on that 13 to 15 knot range"), which the presenter says may not have hit the market yet, and the X-Rap Magnum Extreme ("probably 13 to 15 knots", introduced as "something maybe slightly slower or just a different presentation"). The Mad Mac is named but assigned NO speed anywhere in the video; its only number is fish grade, "from 30 lb all the way to 250". No "200"/full-size variant is named either. The quotation also drops two hedges — actual wording is "these baits are great for locating fish and **also** finding those bites when **maybe** you don't have the conditions to put that kite up". Load-bearing: 13–15 kt is this note's headline parameter and has propagated to lures/mad-mac.md ("The Madmacs 200 speed-trolls at 13–15 kt (`z1CmWHqe9uk`)") and techniques/speed-trolling.md (frontmatter `retrieve_speed`), so the same attribution needs checking in all three |
| species/bluefin-tuna-trolling.md | ~8 kt is the floor below which the kite will not fly at all, so the play becomes a full-size Mad Mac at 13–15 kt | contradicted-by-source | `z1CmWHqe9uk`, `2y0VznL2qk8` | neither cited source supports a kite fly/won't-fly floor. `z1CmWHqe9uk` contains no wind figure at all — "8" and "knot" never co-occur; its only speeds are the 13–15 kt band. `2y0VznL2qk8` uses 8 kt as a WIND split between two kite presentations, and explicitly flies a kite BELOW it: "there are many many days where we're out there on the water and we have less than eight knots of wind ... i'll put out my dead flying fish ... i will put them up in the air underneath the kite and the balloon". So under 8 kt the kite still flies; what changes is trolled flyer vs dead-boat dead flyer. Load-bearing: this is the note's central troll-vs-kite gate, it is also the front-matter `conditions_window` ("wind under ~8 kt (kite will not fly)"), and the note's own ~8 kt paragraph describes kiting under 8 kt two sentences earlier. Cameron to say whether a genuine kite wind floor exists and what it is |
| species/bluefin-tuna-trolling.md | troll sweeping arcs rather than straight-lining away because tuna move at low single-digit mph and the school is not fleeing | contradicted-by-source | `HzE4FHHcvTk` | the arcs, the anti-straight-line advice and the prop-wash rule are all supported verbatim, but the stated MECHANISM is not: the source never gives a tuna swim speed. It argues from the BOAT's speed as an upper bound — "if you troll at 12 knots ... in 15 minutes you can be four miles away from wherever those tuna were", "those two [tuna] are not moving 12 miles in an hour" — and the nearest thing to "the school is not fleeing" is "they're not moving great distances". A bound of "under 12 mph" has been hardened into "low single-digit mph", and the units in the source mix knots with miles. The doctrine stands on its own; only its why is over-stated — **ext-verify**: published cruising and burst swim speeds for Pacific bluefin tuna, and whether a low-single-digit-mph school cruise is the right figure behind the sweeping-arc rule |
| species/bluefin-tuna-trolling.md | one SoCal kite-and-flyline program lands six fish in the time a flat-fall boat lands one | contradicted-by-source | `JiJYamo0QHk` | two mismatches with the transcript. (1) The program is mis-named: the captain says "on my boat we don't do the flat fall thing **we don't throw bait**", and flylining IS throwing bait; the word flyline never appears. What he describes is a kite-skipped flyer at 8.5 kt — "we put it on a kite and we put a way out there to where you got to look with binoculars to see it", "it has to skip on the water" — and he separately dismisses the balloon/dead-flyer style as "catfish fishing". (2) The ratio is aspirational, not achieved: "we want to catch six in the time you catch your one", said in the same breath as the flat-fall rejection, and "catch", not "land". The existing single-source row on this claim (Standing) covers the corroboration question; this row covers the wording |
| species/bluefin-tuna-trolling.md | the diver/swimmer row runs the DTX Minnow 165 and the Husky Magnum on 60–100 lb because swimmer action dies on heavy line | contradicted-internal | cameron | lures/dtx-minnow.md agrees at 60–100 lb, but lures/rapala-husky-magnum.md — linked from the same table cell — specs that lure at the "lighter 40–50 lb class" in both its prose and its spec table, and tackle/gear-classes.md gives the diver-troll class as 30–80 lb. | species/bluefin-tuna-trolling.md | the speed-troll terminal is a low-profile 200 lb bite leader class, crimped, with no bulky swivel on the connection | contradicted-internal | `z1CmWHqe9uk` | the 200 lb, the crimping and the low-profile rule are all verbatim in the source ("this is seagar Blue Label 200 lb", "for Speed each trolling in particular it's really important to have the lowest profile connection you can"), but lures/mad-mac.md specs the same crimped, swivel-free Madmacs 200 connection at "100 lb wind-on, crimped, no swivel" — 100 lb against this note's 200 lb, with neither note distinguishing bite leader from top shot, and rigging/bite-leaders.md is 130 lb base and says it is "the jig-fishing terminal, not a troll rig". Separately the no-swivel rule is narrower than stated: the source DOES crimp a Rosco barrel swivel at the main-line end and rejects only bulky snap swivels; swivel-free applies to the lure end — "you want this tight to the actual lure here you don't want any sort of swivel on this end this is the most important part of this whole rigging" |
| species/bluefin-tuna-trolling.md | at 14 kt the fuel cost runs roughly a quarter gallon per mile, the worst burn rate on a private boat | unverifiable | `bM2vUS1B-yQ` | the note reproduces the seminar faithfully — "it's the worst thing you can do on your yacht or on your private boat because that's your worst fuel burn you could possibly have you're getting like a quarter of a gallon a mile" — but the figure as spoken is 4 miles per gallon, which is good economy for a sportfisher, not a worst case; the speaker plausibly meant a quarter MILE per gallon. That row noted the router and its evidence file were out of a gazetteer unit's scope; the router is now flagged inline. `species/evidence/cabrilla.md` still labels the same trip BOLA in its Provenance and Finding-them entries and in the Zone material list, which a cross-note pass closes |
| species/cabrilla.md | the foam school "roughly **30 fish**, every one above 5 lb and **running 5–15 lb**", worked "under booby birds and gulls" | contradicted-by-source | `0dNfsle54Wo` | the range is hardened. Actual wording: "freaking 30 of them swirling on the surface like they were a school of carp… every single one of them above five pounds on everything, like five to ten, there were some 15s, I'm pretty sure I saw a 20" — a five-to-ten school with some 15s, not a 5–15 lb band. The seagulls belong to a later, separate stop ("see the seagulls, oh I just got swirled too", 17:05); only boobies are on this one. Numerically precise and it is the note's only stated cabrilla grade for surface fish |
| species/cabrilla.md | cabrilla out-produced yellowtail "at a Bahía de los Ángeles tournament in **May**… on the day the yellows went off" | contradicted-by-source | `6P34jI9q1tE` | the source hedges and states no month: "the cabrilla fishing was better than the yellowtail fishing today. Yellows were just a little bit off." May comes from the companion episode (`T3cEQUqiH8E`, "It's May 13th today"), which the note does not cite here. "Went off" also reads in SoCal usage as fish coming ON the bite, so the note's phrasing can invert the source's meaning for the reader it is written for. The claim is load-bearing: it is the note's whole Presence-≠-bite argument |
| species/cabrilla.md | "a dedicated cabrilla program ignores yellowtail and **dorado** entirely" | contradicted-by-source | `EmZO8QiOfik` | the transcript is "we don't chase the Yellow Tail, we don't chase bagas, all we do is chase cabria" — the second species is an unresolved auto-caption token, not dorado. Dorado appears elsewhere in the same transcript only in the BYCATCH list ("Dorado, lots of, right against the Rocks"), so the note has read the bycatch species back into the ignore list, which is the reverse of what the source says about it |
| species/cabrilla.md | "Once the sun clears the hills and light penetrates… the fish turn finicky", and the 170 → 150 downsize keyed to that moment | contradicted-by-source | `EmZO8QiOfik`, `M8hOYQ_6rSg` | both sources tie finicky to the MIDDLE OF THE DAY — "gets bit better middle of the day when the fish are a little bit more finicky" — while the colour switch is what is keyed to the sun clearing the hills, and the size downsize is keyed to a clock time: "past like 9 or 10:00 in the morning you need to drop down size to the 150." The note fuses three separate points on the clock into one trigger. `lures/jerkbaits.md` states the same downsize correctly ("After roughly 9–10 am"), so the two notes now read differently on the same parameter |
| species/cabrilla.md | "a separate Bahía de los Ángeles trip kept one or two [cabrilla] between two anglers and released everything else" | contradicted-by-source | `wX7jUycIJ-U` | the source is "I think we only kept one cabrilla, Mike kept one or two, but other than that we released all the other ones" — two to three fish, doubly hedged, and the boat carried five named anglers (Jordan, Mike, Arthur, Matt, guide Joel), not two. The release ethic itself is sound and corroborated by `T3cEQUqiH8E`; the count and the party size are the finding |
| species/cabrilla.md | "a cabrilla panga session bled its catch at midday **before running in**" | contradicted-by-source | `ZYAILGyrkRk` | the bleed happened at the midday return to the mothership for lunch, and the crew fished an afternoon session afterwards: "it's about 12 o'clock, we're bleeding all the fish right now, we come in for lunchtime, I think we get back out there right after lunch." The doctrine the bullet teaches (bleed at the boat, not at day's end) survives; "running in" reads as an end-of-day return and is not what the footage shows |
| species/cabrilla.md | "Cabrilla is the leopard grouper of the Sea of Cortez" — one common name treated as one species | contradicted-internal | `dEPuDrhoClM`, `n4nMCLLW3tY`, `4Dn7yChmhk0` | the identification is verbatim ("when I say cabrilla it is a leopard grouper, so they are a grouper"), but the corpus records more than one fish under the name: a Loreto fish is called out on camera as "a different type of cabrilla" (`n4nMCLLW3tY`), `locations/loreto.md` carries a *vaqueta* and a grouper named separately in the same dinner rundown, and a distinct GOLDEN colour morph is landed alongside the leopards at BOLA (`4Dn7yChmhk0`, `locations/bahia-de-los-angeles.md`). The note's `scientific_name` is `unknown`, so nothing pins the router to one species while the title and lead assert one — **ext-verify**: whether "cabrilla" in the Sea of Cortez covers one species or several, whether leopard grouper is the right binomial for the fish this note routes, and whether the golden morph is a colour phase of the same fish or a separate species |
| species/calico-bass.md | calico are "current-driven and **tide-indifferent**", stated in Doctrine & conflicts and again in the lead as "planned off the current read, not the tide clock" | contradicted-by-source | `0KQ--N5TjqE`, `YIABTTYXeqc`, `kwMIgkCtFUE` | none of the three cites mentions tide at all. `0KQ--N5TjqE` gives current DIRECTION only ("current direction that you're going to want to look for is either uphill or downhill"), and `kwMIgkCtFUE` is a submerged-structure episode whose only contribution is "when the current runs fish have to point into it" — no kelp posture, no parallel-current rule. The corpus's only ignore-the-tide statement is Jimmy Decker's in `ZoIzpyyS9xw` ("forget what they tell you about the tide, the moon, the wind"), two bullets below under a different heading. The parallel/into/off ranking itself is verbatim and stands; the tide half of the contrast is the finding, and it is load-bearing because the lead builds the whole spotted-bay-bass contrast on it |
| species/calico-bass.md | "the Baja Pacific islands from Cedros down to La Bocana give up 4–9 lb fish at volume" | contradicted-by-source | `NGxyOlPx3ug`, `qri15R3caYE` | the weights are verbatim in both — `NGxyOlPx3ug` has "nine Pounders 8 Pounders 7 Pounders" plus repeated four- and five-pounders, `qri15R3caYE` has "nine pounds calico bass" and "five pounders one after the other" — but neither transcript is Cedros. `NGxyOlPx3ug` (2015) is a skiff trip at "Dono/Donal Island" in Mexican waters and `qri15R3caYE` (2018) is La Bocana. The Cedros end of the stated span has no cite on this sentence. The grade and the volume stand; the geographic anchor is the finding |
| species/calico-bass.md | "track temperature and colour spot to spot and take the warmer, better patch" | contradicted-by-source | `0KQ--N5TjqE` | the tracking habit is verbatim ("track your water temperature from spot to spot... track your watercolor") but the preference is explicitly disclaimed in the same passage: "it doesn't always have to be warm... keep tabs on if the fish are biting in cleaner water dirtier water." The note hardens a stated non-preference into a selection rule, and it sits directly under the note's 65–68 °F band |
| species/calico-bass.md | direct quote "hit three or four sides of it and you know in two or three casts" | contradicted-by-source | `0KQ--N5TjqE` | the string is not in the transcript. What is there is "you can hit four sides of it three sides of it", with the two-or-three-casts count not adjacent to it. The doctrine the quote illustrates IS verbatim — "if there is a b[it]ing bass on this kel[p] it's going to be right on the front end of it" — so the leading-edge rule is untouched; the quotation marks are the finding. Same class as the `qri15R3caYE` "every single drop" row above |
| species/calico-bass.md | sonar signature: "typical working depths run the **outside kelp edge ~50 ft in to ~25 ft**" | unverifiable | — | no cite on the sentence, no matching figure anywhere else in the KB, and nothing in this note's evidence file under "Finding them (sign & sonar)" (which carries only `P6Slg6RQiXw` and `tJFSV3AcIdE`, neither with depths). The number is nevertheless promoted into the `sonar_depth` front-matter infobox field, where the skill build and any future MCP will read it as sourced |
| species/calico-bass.md | "calico will rise **10–15 ft off 80–100 ft of reef** to eat a crankbait or sunk iron" | contradicted-internal | — | the two numbers are recombined from `techniques/inshore-crankbaits.md`, where they are unrelated: "DD-class divers run ~10–15 ft down and get bit on the rise" is a LURE running depth, and "calicos crank off the **80–100 ft** lanes at the islands (Clemente / Catalina)" is a lane depth. Neither states how far a calico rises off the bottom, and the crank note's own `depth_band` caps DD divers at ~13–14 ft actual in saltwater. Also carried into the `sonar_depth` infobox field |
| species/calico-bass.md | the tube-bait router row: a tube bait on an Owner sled weedless rig, 8 ft parabolic-bend bass class, 40 lb+ for 3–4 oz baits, routed to `techniques/tube-bait-fishing.md` | contradicted-by-source | `0HwYl0HO2tc` | the word "tube" never appears in the transcript. The Owner weedless sled head for "fishing grass or heavy kill [kelp]", the 8 ft parabolic rod ("parabolic Bend very important with these baits"; a stiff rod means "the [hook] does fade out of his mouth every time") and the 40 lb fluoro floor ("you don't fish these on anything less than 40... I've started with 25 and there's been tons of [break-offs]") are all verbatim — but they are specified for a **Hookup Baits glide bait**, pitched at a Fred Hall booth by Chad, the bait's own designer/creator. Every parameter checks out; the bait class they are attached to, the technique note the row routes to, and footnote [a]'s framing of the rod rule as a tube-bait rule do not. This re-scopes the existing single-source row on the same claim |
| species/calico-bass.md | the boiler-calico swimbait row: calico "**boiling** on bait on top", 7–9 ft bass class with a 200-size reel, "more aggressive fish and bigger baits as the water warms in July–August" | contradicted-by-source | `lP6cg4eEU6s` | three separate mismatches in one row. (1) "Boiler" in the seminar means a boiler ROCK, not fish boiling on bait — "big boiler Calico situation less kelp more on the open side"; the clarity-edge read ("that's I thought line I want to work right there what my clarity lines") and the committed-fish selection ("I'm looking for the fish that is locked loaded and ready to go") are verbatim, but the surface-boil situation the row is built around is not in the source. (2) The July–August passage is scoped to **spotted bay bass** and "the bait is bigger in the bay" means forage size, not lure size. (3) The rods named are 7'3"–7'9" ("733", "7 foot 6", "794"); 9 ft is never stated, only "a rod that can accommodate 200 size reels". The seminar is promotional (Sav-On Tackle / iRod, speaker "Jeremy", prototypes and pricing pitched on camera), which caps it at low confidence under the registry rubric. The `swimbaits.md` anchor and `species/calico-bass-swimbaits.md` inherit the same boil framing — cluster pass |
| species/calico-bass.md | "rub your hands together to build a light lather or foam, then rinse the fish off before it goes back — a slime-coat step ahead of release" | contradicted-by-source | `KTsXdQXAnkU` | the sequence is inverted. The transcript releases the fish FIRST and then washes the angler: "throw them over the side. Simply rub your hands together, build up a little lather... Get nice and foamy. Rinse them right off." Nothing is applied to the fish, nothing is rinsed off the fish, and no slime coat is mentioned anywhere — yet it sits in Landing & handling as a care instruction a reader would perform on a fish. The lower-lip grip in the bullet above it IS verbatim ("Grab them by the lower lip because every part of them hurts") |
| species/calico-bass.md | "Guided days run at 99% release" and "a big fish is often not weighed at all" | contradicted-by-source | `P6Slg6RQiXw`, `_r_qKX_7080` | both rates generalize one on-camera instance each. `P6Slg6RQiXw` scopes 99% to that single trip — "we released 99% of the fish that we caught and it's just phenomenal fishery" — not to guided days as a class. `_r_qKX_7080` is one 7 lb fish: "Captain just said that's 7 lbs we're not going to weigh him we're going to get him back in the water slow to grow so let him go", which does not carry "often". The release ETHIC underneath is well corroborated (`usHl-4SfqDA` slogan, `I-QBxuV2p7M` "we're letting most go especially the big ones", `SdwwpQMJEOI` weigh-in-then-release, `tJFSV3AcIdE`); the two quantified rates are what is hardened |
| species/calico-bass.md | calico take roughly **5–7 years to reach legal size** | contradicted-by-source | `LqW32NSkObY` | the source pins the figure to a different size than this note's own Regulations section: "they take about 5 to 7 years old just to grow up to a legal size if or about 12 in". The same speaker elsewhere gives the minimum as 14 in, and the note's Regulations section states 14 in (corroborated four ways). So 5–7 years is growth to ~12 in, not to the 14 in legal minimum, meaning the real time-to-legal is longer than stated, not shorter. The note presents this figure as the arithmetic behind the release ethic. ext-verify |
| species/calico-bass.md | "Four panelists converged on it independently" (the bigger-bait / bigger-fish tradeoff) | contradicted-by-source | `ZoIzpyyS9xw` | the panel is four (Benny Florentino, Jimmy Decker, Jack Saul, Corey Sandon) but only three state it. Jack Saul answers the big-calico question "it's pure luck" and endorses a SMALL bait — "it's about half that's five five inch it really works great" — a dissent inside the cite, on the exact axis the doctrine turns on. Benny also prefaces his answer "I'm going to have to agree with all these guys", so the convergence is not fully independent. Load-bearing: this sentence is what lifts the trophy doctrine out of single-panel status, and the dissent is currently invisible to the reader. The quote and the 7–10 lb / 8–10 in / 1–4 lb figures around it are all verbatim and stand |
| species/calico-bass.md | the gray-light window "decid[es] a large share of SoCal bass-tournament results, including a 30-minute giant-bass frenzy right at first light", applying "to wall fishing as well as open kelp and reef" | contradicted-by-source | `RUuPNpK0x-s` | both figures are tied to the tournament clock, not to first light: "a lot of them have been won in the first hour of the tournament" and "a small window where it bit for 30 minutes of nothing but giants". A tournament's first hour is not necessarily first light. Reef is never mentioned; only "that goes for fishing the wall" is stated. The hour-before-sunrise / hour-before-sunset rule in the same bullet IS verbatim ("the general rule of thumb is an hour before sunrise and an hour before sunset") and stands |
| species/calico-bass.md | the Cedros mid-day-filler row and its quote *"it's raining calicos"* | contradicted-by-source | `IdMJnlg_rZk`, `2cxeg_AE418` | the double cite reads as corroboration for what is actually one fused claim across two different days. The play is Day 1 only (`IdMJnlg_rZk`) and carries no mid-day timing — the yellowtail slowed "within the first 2 hours", then "we headed in to catch some Calico bass... just wait for the yell to pop up again", with a "small righteous 5 1/2 in" iron "here in tight on the beach". The quote is Day 2 (`2cxeg_AE418`), spoken in "68 ft water" during a mixed calico/yellowtail bite, not on an in-tight beach retreat. The play and the abundance claim are each sourced; their joining and the mid-day scoping are not. The yellows-off-switch-to-calico logic is independently corroborated by `I-QBxuV2p7M` |

| species/calico-bass.md | Cedros Island 100% catch-and-release for calico, grouper and black seabass — an operator agreement, not a Mexican federal or state regulation | single-source | `ZBRSB4iwtbU`, `ldVj0BoB-kE`, `Zo92MG459gQ` | **no finding — recorded for external verification only, no inline flag.** Verified as one Jose (Cedros Outdoor Adventures) interview re-cut across three uploads (headers 2023-03-26, 2023-05-24, 2023-10-16), exactly as the note already discloses, and the earliest upload does date it on-or-before 2023-03-26. "100% catch and release" and "all the local companies of cedos Island decided" are verbatim; the operator-vs-government distinction is inferred from "it's our fishery... no one else is going to tell us what to do" rather than stated outright. Current status of the agreement is the open question. Regulatory claim. ext-verify |
| species/calico-bass.md | calico temperature preference band **65–68 °F**, with fish going lethargic and dropping to cooler water past ~70 °F while still hitting top-water | single-source | `LqW32NSkObY` | **no finding — recorded for external verification only, no inline flag.** Verbatim in the source ("I'd like to keep it around 65. 70 becomes a little too warm... a little lethargic and they'll start going down to the cooler waters. Biology claim. ext-verify |
| species/calico-bass.md | calico do not migrate; they are pelagic spawners whose recruits settle at the islands or coastal grounds where they hatched, moving only structure pile to structure pile | single-source | `LqW32NSkObY` | **no finding — recorded for external verification only, no inline flag.** Verbatim ("calicos do not migrate... | species/calico-bass.md | calico aggregate to spawn in summer alongside black seabass and grouper, and pressure on those summer aggregations caused the Cedros collapse | single-source | `ZBRSB4iwtbU`, `ldVj0BoB-kE`, `Zo92MG459gQ` | **no finding — recorded for external verification only, no inline flag.** Verbatim ("the Calico the Blacky and the grouper they gather right now in the summer to to spawn and fertilize"; catches down from "by the hundred" to "none or very few"), but the three cites are one re-cut interview, so this is effectively single-source — and it is the mechanism behind both the Spawning section and the Cedros release agreement. Biology claim. ext-verify |

In `JiJYamo0QHk` it is singular and rhetorical — "the lobster fishermen had the greatest year of lobster fishing they've ever had AT THE BEGINNING OF THE SEASON", offered to rebut "the hoopers took all the lobsters" — so "best seasons" (plural) overstates one partial season. | species/california-spiny-lobster.md | a lobster covering the whole ring of a 36 in hoop can come back over the rail if the rope stops moving | contradicted-by-source | `Q196GVwVqro` | inverted conditional. The transcript runs the other way — "IF HE DIDN'T STOP that Lobster was holding on for dear life it would have came right back over the rail" — i.e. coming over the rail is the good outcome of NOT stopping; the deckhand did stop and the fish "almost went into the water". The never-stop rule itself is verbatim ("do not stop pulling only stop when it's in the boat") and the 36 in ring is verbatim; only the causal direction of the failure mode is reversed. It is Justin Botrell's boat rule, not Hansen's |
| species/california-spiny-lobster.md | shorts go over the side immediately after measuring from the bucket | contradicted-internal | `cEscIy278ew`, `EiItVWqFMYc` | `EiItVWqFMYc` — same channel, same speaker — does the opposite: "I don't throw the shorts over, I throw them back in the hoop net, throw them back down on the bottom", on the stated reasoning that a lobster able to crawl out of a net is not in possession and that "nothing attracts a crowd like a crowd". That practice is carried in techniques/hoop-netting.md while this note states over-the-side-immediately as the rule, so the KB asserts both and reconciles neither. The legal reading (not in possession) is also a regulatory claim in its own right. Regulatory claim. ext-verify |
| species/dorado.md | the dorado care doctrine — no bleed, ice immediately, never freshwater-rinse | contradicted-by-source | `E4vKwRaRueA` | `E4vKwRaRueA` ("Dorado Fishing Tactics") contains no fish-care content whatsoever — it ends at the hookup ("toss them right over the side"), and bleeding, icing and rinsing are never mentioned. The doctrine itself is sound and is stated in full by `5to3Q5P7w90` ("you don't even need to pop a kill and bleed them … get them ice quick"; "you don't want to do this with a fresh water hose ever"). This is the outstanding half of the 2026-08-28 fish-care/dorado-and-general.md row, which re-cited that note and recorded species/dorado.md as "not yet fixed" — the cite is the finding, not the claim |
| species/dorado.md | the ~71–72 °F reference band for the green/blue boundary, with 71.6 °F "measured on a productive paddy" | contradicted-by-source | `E4vKwRaRueA` | the source gives exactly one reading — "we got the right temperature of water we're 71.6 degrees" — spoken on arrival at the paddy, BEFORE it was fished, so "on a productive paddy" states an outcome the source had not yet observed. No band, and no productive/unproductive comparison, appears anywhere in the transcript; ~71–72 °F and front-matter `sst_band_f: 71-72` are extrapolated from the single number. `uyjTdgIw-1k` independently reads 71–72 °F on an offshore break out of Mission Bay, which corroborates that such water exists at the break without corroborating it as a dorado preference band. Load-bearing: this is the number a day plan pulls per spot from BightSST — **ext-verify**: the SST range dorado actually associate with off Southern California |
| species/dorado.md | dorado read as boomerang-shaped arcs at 90–120 ft on the fathometer, plus a bait ball packed under the kelp | contradicted-by-source | `ZFqe49jRgA0` | the source attaches 90–120 ft to unspecified marks — "you see some boomerangs down there 90 to 120 feet okay there's some fish there spend a little time there" — after separately saying to look in the water for visible dorado or yellowtail. The depth is species-agnostic "some fish"; the dorado attribution is the KB's. The "bait ball packed under the kelp" half is absent from the transcript entirely. Units are correctly feet (no fathom error). The figure has propagated as dorado-specific into front-matter `sonar_depth`, conditions/kelp-paddies.md, species/dorado-kelp-paddy.md and species/yellowtail.md, so a correction is a cluster-wide edit |
| species/dorado.md | dorado are an upper-water-column fish (the depth doctrine the Cedros bottom fish is contradicted against) | contradicted-by-source | `ZFqe49jRgA0`, `E4vKwRaRueA` | neither cite states a water-column position. `E4vKwRaRueA` says only "they will hang out underneath these kelps they will patrol around the kelps", which locates them at the paddy, not at a depth; `ZFqe49jRgA0` gives the species-agnostic boomerangs above. The upper-column read is a KB inference, which matters because the note frames the `9tIp4n7q850` bottom fish as a documented contradiction TO it and carries the pairing in two sections — **ext-verify**: the depth range dorado actually occupy relative to a drifting kelp paddy |
| species/dorado.md | the Cedros bottom-caught dorado came on a "deep" halibut drift | contradicted-by-source | `9tIp4n7q850` | the catch and the on-camera surprise are verbatim ("way out here on the bottom", "that's amazing"), but no drift depth is stated anywhere in the video; the only depth remark on the trip is the opposite — "we're shallow dude" — spoken earlier in the yellowtail segment. "Deep" is the KB's and does the outlier's whole argumentative work: a bottom fish in shallow water is a much weaker counter to an upper-column read than one in deep water |
| species/dorado.md | dorado feed charging straight ahead, where tunas and yellowtail grab a bait and immediately turn sideways | contradicted-by-source | `E4vKwRaRueA` | hedge hardened. The source's dorado half is explicitly two-way — "they might turn sideways they might not" — before "they're happy to feed in going in a straight forward in a line picking off as many baits as they can". The tuna/yellowtail half is verbatim. The hedge is load-bearing because this contrast is the entire stated mechanism for the J-hook-over-circle call, which the note carries in three places |
| species/dorado.md | "Do not change baits; change the size of what the bait is dragging", and hook size mattering "as much as" leader size | contradicted-by-source | `E4vKwRaRueA` | changing baits never comes up in the transcript, so the prohibition is invented; the source's trigger is "i can see him swimming around the boat but i can't get a bite". On the equivalence, it says "the hook size really matters in a picky bite" and "the same applies with the hooks" — a parallel step-down, not a stated equality of magnitude with leader size. Both the step-down ladder (40→30→20→15) and the match-hook-to-bait rule are verbatim and unaffected |
| species/dorado.md | a pink colour NAMED "Dorado", 1 oz or 5/8 oz, as a named dorado favourite at paddies | contradicted-by-source | `5Oh3IPIC5g4` | in the transcript "Dorado" is the FISH, not a colour: "the 5/8 pink the Dorado absolutely love that pink one … especially on Paddy's stuff". The colours this speaker actually names are mint, sardine, red crab, glow green, white shad and fin bait. The 1 oz / 5/8 oz sizes, the pink preference and the paddy context are all supported — only the colour NAME is the KB's reading. Compounding it, techniques/tube-bait-fishing.md renders the same check-cast from the same cite as a 1 oz bullet in "mint or sardine", so the two notes hand a reader different colours for one action. The existing footnote [c] flags brand/colour confidence generally but not this specific misreading |
| species/dorado.md | the circle-hook dorado "square on the jaw inside the offshore kelp-paddy program itself", the sole counter-example to the J-hook doctrine | contradicted-by-source | `r4J5nP5Bkl4` | the circle-hook callout is verbatim ("circle hook look at that right on the jaw I don't think I've ever had that", on 30 lb test) but the fish is never named on camera — only "he", "a big dude" — so the species rests on the surrounding kelp/dorado stretch, and the immediately following passage shows the crew unable to ID two other fish ("I'm not sure what in the world they are"). The evidence file records the inference honestly; the main note asserts it flatly. The location is separately wrong: the source sequences the backside of San Clemente as the LATER bluefin sundown stop, after the kelp/dorado picking, so the paddy session's place is unstated. Both matter because this is the only corpus counter-example carried against a doctrine the note states in three sections |
| species/dorado.md | "delay boating the first fish … get a second bait in before you land it", cited to three sources | contradicted-by-source | `brx6Ie_L2FM` | the third cite does not state the tactic. `tcso7Lpm_Xs` ("before bringing him in we're got another bait out hopefully there's some followers") and `qM7iOO7fOBw` ("we're not bringing him up cuz we're trying to Chum a few more in if we leave them in the water it attracts more") both support it fully. `brx6Ie_L2FM` supports only the weaker school-follows observation ("you hook a fish they're on you … and then they fish right around you"), shows fish gaffed promptly, and its one instruction runs the other way — throw a bait on a fish being brought IN. The doctrine stands on two independent sources; the finding is the third cite |
| species/dorado.md | a 2/0 J-hook as the baseline size on a sardine for dorado | contradicted-internal | `P9H-bpzT7eU` | species/dorado-kelp-paddy.md makes 1/0 the all-around size on a ~6 in Southern California receiver sardine and reserves 2/0–3/0 for "big donkey sardines" (`E4vKwRaRueA`); tackle/hooks.md carries size 2 and 1–1/0 for kelp-paddy dorado and no 2/0 figure at all. The router therefore publishes as its baseline the size the sub-article reserves for oversized bait, and neither note flags the split. `P9H-bpzT7eU` does state "a 2oj hook", so the cite is sound and the disagreement is between notes. Two smaller cite drifts on the same line: the source never says "flyline" (it says "cast it out there") and gives "a short Top Shot", not a length |
| species/dorado.md | a "pink/silver troll lure (Mad Scad class) for testing paddies" | contradicted-by-source | `VgpgJ8IAyJo` | two errors in one line. The source says pink only — "anything that's got some pink in it, dorado seem to be attracted to" — and the word silver never appears; and the paddy test it describes is a CAST from the tower, not a troll: "I'd throw this from the tower … if we didn't see something come out and chase it … we would just move on to the next one." The note's own router row states the tower cast correctly, so the gear summary contradicts its own note as well as its source. The pink/silver TROLL colour is `E4vKwRaRueA`'s ("anything with pink and silver in it purple and black"), and footnote [d] already routes the troll doctrine there |
| species/dorado.md | the 4–6 lb release encouragement is "a voluntary table-fare practice, not a CDFW size or bag regulation" | unverifiable | `P9H-bpzT7eU` | the source states only the practice — "most boats encourage you to release those little four five six Pounders try to keep some of the bigger ones so you get some fish tacos out of them" — and says nothing about CDFW in either direction. | species/dorado.md | dorado hold as open-water schools below and behind Catalina by the late warm-stable phase, typically September | single-source | `5to3Q5P7w90` | the open-water-school framing is explicit in the source ("found some dorado but down below their open water schools real easy fishing"), so it is not paddy fish misread. The flag is on the generalisation: this is one 2022-09-14 charter report describing that week, promoted to a seasonal-phase rule, and it is the note's only non-San-Diego "where" claim. The location wording is also ASR-mangled ("behind the below cat", after "the backside of cat"), which is what "below and behind Catalina" is reconstructed from — **stands**: single-source is not wrong, and a second September report from the Catalina backside settles it |
| species/ocean-whitefish.md | the lead's "a good one is a legit 30-lb-class dropper-loop fish" | contradicted-by-source | `6-8KfjEg0x8` | the transcript reads "I'm telling you, white like pound for pound, whitefish highly underrated species. No weight is given for any fish on camera; the only size language is "they get freaking big up here. This is kind of an average size one." The existing single-source flag in Where & when compounds it by calling this one of the "30-lb-class claims" — **ext-verify**: the actual maximum grade of ocean whitefish (Caulolatilus princeps), which decides whether a 30 lb fish is even possible |
| species/ocean-whitefish.md | whitefish come off Santa Cruz, Santa Rosa and Santa Barbara Island "on the same shallow 100-250 ft island high spots" | contradicted-by-source | `6-8KfjEg0x8` | the cited day names no individual island — only "the Channel Islands" and "just outside of Ventura" — and gives no whitefish depth at all: the whitefish stop is called only "the shallows," and the one depth figure in the video, 175 ft, belongs to the separate rockfish rock the boat ran to afterwards and is absent from the note. The 100-250 ft band itself is corroborated KB-wide for the rockfish variety program (species/rockfish-lingcod.md, locations/channel-islands.md) and is borrowed here by reference, so the finding is the island list and the whitefish-specific depth, not the band. Two orphan numbers ride on it: front-matter `depth_band: 25-250 ft`, and the Landing & handling barotrauma line's 150-250 ft, which matches neither the note's own 100-250 ft nor any source |
| species/ocean-whitefish.md | "Cortez and Tanner produce a large grade" — "the white fish are absolutely Jurassic" | contradicted-by-source | `Y1xeieQI3B4` | the quote is verbatim ("Yeah, dude. The note's other bank cite `fDSd9kqwYW0` also names Cortez only ("110 miles offshore from Long Beach... out on the Cortez Bank"). The KB's Tanner/Cortes naming tension is already tracked on locations/tanner-bank.md, whose Resolved row records the corpus naming the two interchangeably — this row is the whitefish page's instance of it |
| species/ocean-whitefish.md | a ~150 ft ridge drift at Cortez Bank takes whitefish alongside reds and sheephead on squid | contradicted-by-source | `fDSd9kqwYW0` | every element checks out separately — the ridge and depth verbatim ("a little shallower on this one we're up on the ridge here just on the edge... we're roughly about 150 feet"), the drift, squid as "the name of the game", and all three species on the day — but they are two stops, not one drift. The whitefish and sheephead come at 06:04-09:19, BEFORE the 150 ft ridge call, and the captain's own summary separates them: "we fished out there at the edge of the bank and we ended up catching our limit on the Reds... we moved into the shallows we picked up some sheep head and some white fish." The narrated 150 ft drift produced reds and "sandwich groupers". The same compression is carried on locations/tanner-bank.md's spot table (a ~150 ft ridge drift on the bank's edge listed as producing reds, sheephead and whitefish) |
| species/ocean-whitefish.md | an anchored Catalina front-side calico stop produces whitefish "to about 10 lb" | contradicted-by-source | `HGyL7pXy3Ts` | the anchored-calico context is solid — Catalina, a rock coming up to about 8 ft below the surface in 48-54 ft near the mooring cans, targeting "some white fish and some calico bass" — but the 10 lb figure comes from a single sarcastic aside about one fish ("now you don't think they're so cute after he stoled your 10 pound white fish"), not from a stated grade, so generalising it to a spot grade is a one-fish extrapolation. "Front side" is never said either: the captions render the place as "just inside of what they call white scope," almost certainly Whites Cove/Whites Landing, which IS front side, but by inference rather than statement |
| species/ocean-whitefish.md | "in late October at Catalina they were biting alongside calicos and bonito in what was called steady wintertime fishing" | contradicted-by-source | `OYOda6T3f-8` | whitefish appear in that 2022-10-20 report only inside one second-hand fly-guide sentence — "my buddy Von podmore who runs the salty fly guide service is catching Calico Bonita even Whitefish on flies so I don't know how he's doing the white fish" — while "looks like good steady wintertime fishing over there" summarises the whole Catalina picture (bonito, bass, yellowtail). One guide's fly catch is generalised into a whitefish bite alongside two other species. The island is ASR-garbled as "Kathleen" and read correctly from context. The sentence also carries no year, which the no-relative-time rule wants on a bare month |
| species/ocean-whitefish.md | "once one comes over the rail the rest of the drop is usually more of the same — the reason the program is keep dropping rather than move" | contradicted-by-source | `6-8KfjEg0x8` | the schooling half is verbatim ("And they school up like crazy up here. The only "didn't move" line is the crew's day summary about the OTHER stop — "hit this basically one rock fishing spot. We didn't move very much" — which is the 175 ft rockfish rock, not the whitefish shallows. It is the note's only stated whitefish boat-handling rule |
| species/ocean-whitefish.md | whitefish hold "higher off the bottom and along the sand edge" rather than pinned to the pinnacle — the note's core routing doctrine | contradicted-by-source | `6-8KfjEg0x8`, `fDSd9kqwYW0`, `CKq0Z6ExVs4` | none of the three cites states it. `6-8KfjEg0x8` contains no sand-edge, hard-to-sand or column-position language about whitefish at all, and the adjacent idea in `_ZThckj2TIM` (reds tight to the rock, bocaccio out in the sand off it) says nothing about whitefish. The two days that are supposed to show whitefish sharing a rock with reds show a different geometry: in both the Channel Islands day and the Cortez Bank day the whitefish came off a separate, shallower stop than the reds. The claim is load-bearing — it sets front-matter `sonar_depth`, the Finding-them sonar bullet, the Situations table's column logic and the entire "whitefish route separately from rockfish" rationale in Doctrine & conflicts — and it is propagated unflagged to species/rockfish-lingcod.md ("a tilefish rather than a rockfish, hold higher off the bottom and along the sand edge, and route differently") — **ext-verify**: whether ocean whitefish (Caulolatilus princeps) characteristically hold off the bottom and along sand/hard-bottom margins rather than tight to structure, and whether the family placement the note asserts uncited (Malacanthidae, not Sebastes) is correct |
| species/ocean-whitefish.md | "the eat comes on the drop rather than after a soak" | contradicted-by-source | `6-8KfjEg0x8` | the source supports "fast" only: he describes the dropper loop and the sliding sinker, then "And he's on. And we're bit. Just like that." Nothing distinguishes an eat on the drop from an eat after a soak, and the drop-versus-soak framing is the operational half of the bullet a reader would act on. The fast-eat behaviour itself is independently corroborated by `8XIy7hPsFkQ` ("right when it hits the bottom, start winding. Don't let it sit there... Catch a white fish"), so the finding is the added distinction, not the speed |
| species/ocean-whitefish.md | whitefish paint as "scattered mid-column marks and arches over hard bottom and along the sand edge" — the "scratchy marks coming through throughout the column" | contradicted-by-source | `6-8KfjEg0x8` | the quote is an accurate substring ("We have some kind of scratchy marks coming through throughout the column") but the speaker never attributes those marks to whitefish — he says it while explaining why he is adding a sliding sinker — and "arches", "hard bottom" and "sand edge" occur nowhere in the transcript. This bullet is the sole source for the front-matter `sonar_depth` field, which a day plan reads as a species signature, and for the note's claim that whitefish sit higher than the rockfish stack (ledgered separately above) |
| species/ocean-whitefish.md | "the read that says whitefish" — stacked willing mid-column marks biting immediately, versus the picking one-at-a-time bottom contact of a lingcod stop | contradicted-by-source | `6-8KfjEg0x8` | no whitefish-versus-lingcod mark or bite contrast exists in the video. Lingcod is mentioned only as a fish that was caught and measured short ("This is a California lingcod... this is definitely a short"). The whole comparison is synthesised, and it is the bullet that tells a reader how to tell a whitefish stop from a lingcod stop on the meter — the acceptance-test question for the Finding-them section |
| species/ocean-whitefish.md | whitefish "eat a wound iron" on an island high spot | contradicted-by-source | `UfuiWFVvz2E` | the whitefish is real ("An angry whitefish") and yo-yoing is in play about 30 s earlier ("I'm yo-yoing"), but the transcript never says the whitefish ate the iron — the link is caption adjacency — and no retrieve detail ("wound") is given anywhere. The setting is wrong twice over: it is the Cortez Bank high spot in 120 ft during a yellowtail bite, an offshore BANK rather than an island, while the row header reads "Working an island high spot with iron". The behaviour the row actually rests on — a jig left sitting on the bottom gets picked up by whitefish and small cod — is separately and cleanly sourced to `8XIy7hPsFkQ` |
| species/ocean-whitefish.md | the island bottom program is a 30 lb bait-class dropper-loop rig "on a level-wind reel" | contradicted-by-source | `6-8KfjEg0x8` | no reel of any kind is described in the transcript. The dropper loop ("I'm putting down a dropper loop"), the squid tank ("We have a tank full of squid") and the 30 lb rig class are all on camera; "level-wind" is not, and it appears twice under this cite — the Situations table's first row and the Gear summary's rod/reel bullet. If it was read off on-screen footage rather than speech it cannot carry a bare cite |
| species/ocean-whitefish.md | whitefish are "a table fish, not a throwback — pound for pound highly underrated eating" | contradicted-by-source | `6-8KfjEg0x8` | the source says "pound for pound, whitefish highly underrated species" and never mentions eating, meat or table quality; the only "very tasty" line in the video is about the blue rockfish at the separate rockfish stop. The quoted fragment is accurate in both places it is used, but the "on the table" gloss in Doctrine & conflicts and the "highly underrated eating" plus "the value is table quality, not fight" expansion in Landing & handling are the KB's insertion, and they are the only reason the note tells a reader to keep the fish |
| species/ocean-whitefish.md | a ~10 lb whitefish was taken off the line by a sea lion at Catalina, and one was saved only by getting it aboard quickly | contradicted-by-source | `HGyL7pXy3Ts` | one garbled passage carries both readings in the same breath — "we have a big white fish and the sea lions after him... we got it away just in time sea lion was right after him ready to take him... now you don't think they're so cute after he stoled your 10 pound white fish" — so the transcript establishes neither two fish nor a lost one. The only unambiguous theft comes later and names no species: "he got it the sea lion got it right there sea lion got it broke you off." The evidence file already carries an asr-uncertain marker on landed-versus-lost; the main note presents the two-fish reconciliation as settled. The handling rule itself is well sourced ("you better wind fast Brian that sea lions on you like a hobo on a hot dog") |
| species/ocean-whitefish.md | California/CDFW — a 10-fish whitefish allowance stacks on top of the 10-rockfish daily bag, "you can keep 10 whitefish with your 10 rockfish" | single-source | `iAr6sbRC384` | one 2020 Fred Hall seminar (Bobby Martinez, "Modern Rockfishing Methods", uploaded 2020-03-16 — so the note's as-of 2020-03 is right) is the corpus's only source. Three drifts on top of that: the captions render the quote as "you can keep 10 whitefish with your 10 / walk fish", so "10 rockfish" is a silent ASR normalisation (defensible, the preceding line says "it's 10 rockfish"); "stacks on top of" is read from "with your"; and the note's "stated from recollection at a live seminar" is an editorial read of the off-the-cuff register — the transcript carries no hedge and asserts it as current law ("as far as your bag limit don't forget it's 10 rockfish..."). The note's asr-uncertain marker on the surrounding sub-limit detail is correct; verbatim it is "canary and their rockfish 3 change it up they they open / it up to 1 now there's 30 sheep it's / wide but the dead sheep in the quad". The same claim is carried on species/rockfish-lingcod.md and sources/regulatory-claims.md — **ext-verify**: the CURRENT CDFW daily bag limit for ocean whitefish and whether it sits inside or outside the rockfish/groundfish aggregate; a regulatory claim, five years stale |
| species/ocean-whitefish.md | "in California the whitefish bite opens with the groundfish season", and the season dates plus the depth-based Rockfish Conservation Area line gate whitefish too | contradicted-by-source | `46ha7J1Fc6A` | the source records only one boat's current program just after the opener — "currently right now the rock fish season opener just started so we're fishing rockfish lingcod whitefish and sheephead" (Bubba at the Channel Islands Sportfishing booth, Fred Hall 2020) — and never says whitefish ACCESS is gated by the groundfish season, and never mentions an RCA depth line. Both the Where & when presence-is-not-access sentence and the whole second Regulations bullet rest on that inference, asserted as sourced regulatory fact. Separately, species/evidence/ocean-whitefish.md calls the speaker "a San Diego charter operator"; the transcript names the Channel Islands Sportfishing booth, which is Oxnard/Ventura — **ext-verify**: whether CDFW's groundfish season dates and RCA depth closures actually apply to ocean whitefish, or whether the species is open year-round and at any depth |
| species/opah.md | opah "eat a jig or a sinker-weighted bait fished 150–200 ft down while the boat drifts" | contradicted-by-source | `tpmOYXYQwhU` | the depth, the unit and the drift are all verbatim ("they get down you know 150 to 200 feet", "you're drifting along"), but the presentation is not: the source says "somebody goes deep and uses either a sinker **or a lure or something like that**". The word jig never appears anywhere in this answer (its only two occurrences in the transcript, 00:29:04 and 00:34:19, are unrelated surface-jig talk), and "sinker-weighted bait" collapses a sinker-OR-lure disjunction into one rig. The note's own evidence file and the Situations table row 2 both quote it correctly — only the lead hardens it, which is the sentence a reader takes as the deep-drop rig |
| species/opah.md | "both sources class opah as an incidental catch rather than plannable" (Where & when, and again in Doctrine & conflicts) | contradicted-by-source | `tio1oeibVlM`, `tpmOYXYQwhU` | only `tpmOYXYQwhU` classifies anything — "in our experience that it's it's an incidental catch", answering Howard's question of whether opah are always incidental or sometimes targeted. `tio1oeibVlM` never uses the word, never ranks opah against a target, and its register is celebratory rather than bycatch-framed; the entire basis is the contrast in "drop that jig down deep… when everybody's back there fishin' that tuna". It is load-bearing because the two-source agreement is what the Doctrine & conflicts section rests on, and one of the two sources is silent |
| species/opah.md | "Catalina, offshore is the only water the corpus names for opah" | unverifiable | `tio1oeibVlM` | the captions give "seven different species they've just found out **Catalina offshore** we've been leading the pack and sustainability" — an unpunctuated fragment wedged between a taxonomy statement and a sponsor/sustainability plug, with no verb attaching it to opah, to a catch, or to a fishery. Nothing else in the 54-second clip names a place. The reading is load-bearing three times over: the Where & when section, the Catalina zone-guide stub, and the `sources/spot-harvest.md` row that harvests "Catalina, offshore" as a zone. Carried flagged rather than removed |
| species/opah.md | Situations row 1's conditions — "boat stopped and drifting on tuna, chum and flylined baits off the stern" | contradicted-by-source | `tio1oeibVlM` | the source gives exactly one clause of context, "when everybody's back there fishin' that tuna". The boat is never described as stopped or drifting, and chum and flylined baits appear nowhere in the clip; both are imported from the general SoCal tuna-drift picture. Restated as a hydraulic claim it reads as a factual disagreement with the Landesfeind position above it, when what the two sources actually differ over is what to DO in the same window — which is exactly what the bullet's own decision frame ("whether you can change presentation") says the choice turns on |

The through-summer end of the window and the `season_peak: mar…aug` field are extensions. The corpus's own dated white seabass records cluster July–August 2022, which supports summer but is a different evidentiary basis than a cited seasonal doctrine — **ext-verify**: the California market-squid spawning season at the Channel Islands / Catalina and the white seabass season shape, to settle whether the extension is right even though it is uncited |

Count, island, new moon and warm water are all verbatim; the squid is supplied, and it is the leg that makes the day read as the note's squid-driven program rather than an unexplained moon bite. New moon was 2022-08-27; the report is 2022-08-31 and dates the day only as "the other day" |

The transcript also attributes the idea to Sean Morgan and hedges it on camera ("I don't know, he's got a lot of marlin, so… it does make sense"), where the note states it flat. The "very tidal bite," the marlin/bluefin-foamer parallel and the hour-before-the-change rule are all verbatim and stand |
| species/white-seabass.md | the current-direction doctrine conflict — parallel-to-structure as the default prior against a late-fall into-the-island exception | contradicted-by-source | `Z3rZqy2Pi8E`, `6TBxHnkYXI0` | neither leg is a white seabass statement. `Z3rZqy2Pi8E` never mentions white seabass: the trip is Sunday 2022-11-20 to the San Clemente Island East End backside, the catch is "really good bass fishing and some bonita," and the off-color/into-the-island read is given for those fish — "we narrowed it down to a few spots that had off-color water and current running in towards the beach in the kelp, they're pretty good on the bass." `6TBxHnkYXI0`'s parallel prior is framed as a general structure rule about bait orientation and leading edges ("you need current running in a direction that's going to be parallel to the structure"), worked through calico bass and carrying its own exception for standalone offshore banks like Farnsworth. Both reads may hold for white seabass; nothing in the corpus says so, and the note builds a species doctrine conflict — plus a situations-table row — on two non-seabass statements |
| species/white-seabass.md | "fishing inside a Catalina MLPA is closed to the public" | single-source | `Y2bXn44lfqo` | the closure is inferred; the transcript states only the access side — "because the permit that we have, we're allowed to fish in MLPAs, like these broodstock, so it's a dedicated trip just for sea bass fishing" — and asks "now, isn't the MLPA start like right inside." Nothing on camera says an MLPA is closed to the public, and California MPA designations differ by site (SMR vs SMCA), with some allowing limited take. | species/yellowtail.md | the fall-fragmentation regime dated October–November, with island fish still biting while the water holds and paddy yellows as bycatch on dorado paddies | contradicted-by-source | `HcEh5KOYTH4`, `CrLDC4O8qS8`, `VWClGAn2WEw` | none of the three names a month, "October", "November" or "fall" — October is inferred from their upload dates (2022-10-12, 2018-10-15, 2022-10-13) and November from none of them. The paddy-yellowtail-with-dorado half is well supported in all three ("two good sized Yellow Tail", "big offshore kelp yellow"). The "island fish keep biting while the water holds" half is absent from all three, which are offshore paddy trips with no island or water-temperature content |
| species/yellowtail.md | Cedros holds resident "home guard" fish — 40+ lb, sometimes 30 lb — in any month | contradicted-by-source | `WE643Fue1_A` | the grades are verbatim ("a true home guard 40 plus pound or 30, even a 30 pounder is going to get you in the rocks"), but the source's scope is spatial, not seasonal: "you have a shot at hooking a home guard yellowtail at any moment any part of the island". No month, season or year-round claim is made; the video is a September 2022 trip-prep tackle rundown. "In any month" is the KB's inference and it is load-bearing in Where & when |
| species/yellowtail.md | San Benito yellowtail sat at 30–40 fathoms feeding right off the bottom | contradicted-by-source | `JaKSGkZ6CAc` | the captain says "they're all feeding on squid 30 40 50 Fathoms deep here… why we're getting them right off the bottom" — 50 fathoms is dropped. Units (fathoms), the mackerel-pushed-the-squid-out causal chain and the stomach-content line all verify, the last one stated more strongly in the source ("every fish we've been bringing up's been having handfuls of fresh squid in them") than in the note |
| species/yellowtail.md | throw a squid-colored jig with glow at squid feeders; find the forage on the meter before writing the zone off | contradicted-by-source | `YZtX1MiT0y8` | the glow/squid-color jig line is a BLUEFIN call at Tanner Bank — "when they're at tanner and they're feeding on squid a squid colored jig with some glow could make a difference in getting bit" — not a yellowtail one, so it sits under the wrong species. The squid-relocation reading beside it IS near-verbatim ("maybe that squid moved out into deeper water… it's not like they left"), but the source's remedy is boats searching the zone again, not reading the meter |
| species/yellowtail.md | a mackerel drifted to the outside edge of a sardine bait ball out-produces a bait in the middle | contradicted-by-source | `d0yGBQDeY_4` | the source records one mackerel on the outside getting eaten — "get on the outside of the bait ball, those yellowtail are surrounding this bait ball… they saw that big nice juicy Mac and they took it" — with no bait fished in the middle for comparison. The larger-item logic is the source's; the comparative "out-produces" is the KB's hardening of a single observation |
| species/yellowtail.md | mackerel spawning peaks late spring–early summer, putting that bait close to shore and spread out | single-source | `0dIwWiOc1NY` | verbatim — "late spring and early summer is peak spawning season for mackerel, which means they're close to shore and spread out all over" — and the season form (not a relative-time phrase) is the source's own. Flagged only because it is the corpus's single statement of a biology parameter that drives bait-making timing. ext-verify |
| species/yellowtail.md | anchor the day to tide and current, not the clock; a Cortez high spot turned on with the incoming tide | contradicted-by-source | `YZtX1MiT0y8`, `UfuiWFVvz2E` | the seen-but-not-biting daily window IS in `YZtX1MiT0y8` ("a little time with bite window that they have each day"), but the cycle it names is LUNAR, not tidal — "now we're getting into it off the lunar cycle this window lay down"; tide/current is substituted. In `UfuiWFVvz2E` the incoming tide is the captain's stated plan ("we're planning on fishing yellowtail… as this high tide starts rolling in"), not an observed turn-on, though a bite does follow on camera. The tide framing is separately supported by `0dIwWiOc1NY` ("the tides were off, too, so we moved on"); note the lunar line sits against this note's own "no corpus source: moon-phase effect" gap flag |
| species/yellowtail.md | hour-specific call of ~5:00–5:45 pm at San Clemente | contradicted-by-source | `V_ONnegk95M` | the times are exact but the period is inverted: the briefing — "nothing happening until about five, five thirty, five forty five something like that, that's when you wanna start soaking baits" — is given the night before for the NEXT DAY's fishing, and that next day opens "little gray light bite, that's what captain jeff was saying". It is a morning window, not a pm one |
| species/yellowtail.md | hard wind (20–25 kt+) after a run of surface days sends the bite to the yo-yo | contradicted-by-source | `ejUTAnONf7o` | the doctrine (wind moves the bite down not off; fleet goes mostly yo-yo; the iron still picks fish) is fully supported — "we've metered it. They're not coming up", "we talked to a couple of the uh boats there, and it was mostly yo-yo", two yellows on the surface iron. The figure is not: the source gives one unitless number, "I think it's pushing 25" / "Gust 25" — never knots, never mph, never a 20–25 range. The same figure is carried in the Situations → techniques row |
| species/yellowtail.md | fish balled tight to the bottom are worth a yo-yo burned past them for the reaction strike | contradicted-by-source | `tpmOYXYQwhU` | `tpmOYXYQwhU` has no bottom-balled school, no burned yo-yo and no reaction strike. Its yo-yo doctrine is the mid-water metered school the note cites it for one bullet earlier — "they don't come up and boil around but just about every lure that makes it down to the depth that that fish is is a bite". The co-cite `HTowqnwAMeA` supports the bottom-balled → dropper-loop half only |
| species/yellowtail.md | on paddies, look for fish flashing under the kelp; a school can also show as bait "shattering" on the surface | contradicted-by-source | `qM7iOO7fOBw` | "shattering" and the yellowtail-school read are verbatim ("look at the bait over there shattering… it's a Yellow Tail school"), but the setting is a La Paz high spot in the Sea of Cortez with mackerel flylined over structure — there is no paddy, no offshore kelp, and nothing about fish flashing under kelp anywhere in the video. Both the paddy framing and the region are the KB's |
| species/yellowtail.md | structure fish mark as arcs and boil-ups at ~5–10 fathoms near pinnacles and high spots | contradicted-by-source | `D5DR7Kx42_A` | the source carries no depth figure at all — no fathoms, no feet — and none of "arcs", "boil-ups", "pinnacles" or "high spots". It supports only "we see tight balls of yellowtails sometimes hugging the structure". Load-bearing: the 5–10 fathom band is also in front-matter `sonar_depth` and in the Situations → techniques row, and it is the note's headline yellowtail sonar signature |
| species/yellowtail.md | deep Baja high-spot fish mark in the 150–300 ft band, stacked on the up-current face | contradicted-by-source | `vqsD0qpwcJA` | "stacked on the up-current face" has no basis — no current-face language appears anywhere; the only stacking line is "yellowtail everywhere on this high spot". The band is a pre-trip expectation, not a marked read: "I think what we're going to be fishing is typically about 150 to 300 feet, that's kind of what I've heard", against a fished "we're fishing 150 180 feet deep". BOLA as the location does verify ("the bay of l.a. and baja which is just legendary yellowtail grounds") |
| species/yellowtail.md | a thin, lighter mark above a bait ball is the sign to commit the anchor over a hard-bottom mark, not the bait pile alone | contradicted-by-source | `6DzbsElGE7E` | inverted. The mark reading verifies ("that lighter blue, that might just be a little piece of that yellow tail, it's pushing that bait around the rock"), but the anchor trigger in the source IS the bait — "I needed enough bait there to know that I need to get anchored up here", "see all that bait, that lets me know that something's going on here" — and he drops the anchor in sand, not on rock: "you want to drop your anchor in that sandy part" |
| species/yellowtail.md | full-size iron (Salas 6X class, 8 oz) in stronger current, 6X Jr (4 oz) when it's light | contradicted-by-source | `D5DR7Kx42_A` | the cite names "heavy full-size jigs like Salas 6X" with no ounce weight, no 6X Jr, and no current-based sizing rule; its only wind/current iron swap is for SURFACE iron ("it might be windy and you need a heavier surface iron… my favorite kind of jig to use is a salus 7x"). Only the co-located flat-fall figure verifies: "vertical Jigs and flat fall like jigs from 100 to 180 grams depending on the current". The same 8 oz / 4 oz pair is repeated in the Gear summary |
| species/yellowtail.md | a Salas 6X is 8 oz and a 6X Jr 4 oz, chosen by current strength | contradicted-internal | `D5DR7Kx42_A` | [techniques/yo-yo-iron.md](../techniques/yo-yo-iron.md) puts the same jig at "roughly 5–6 oz" ("120–150 ft: a Salas 6X, roughly 5–6 oz") and sizes 6X vs 6X Jr by DEPTH bands (60/80/100 ft → 6X Jr; 150–250 ft → full-size 6X), not by current strength. Two notes give the same jig two different weights and two different governing variables |
| species/yellowtail.md | slow-pitch / knife-jig class, 130–250 g | contradicted-by-source | `vqsD0qpwcJA` | the source's packing range is "I'm packing like 130 gram to 200 gram"; 250 g appears only as one specific producing jig ("this 250 like pink and silver wing fall… they just absolutely annihilate this"). 130–250 g is a merged range, not a stated one, and it has propagated into front-matter `gear_classes`, the Gear summary, and techniques/slow-pitch-jigging.md |
| species/yellowtail.md | No. 2 hook as the baseline on a sardine | contradicted-by-source | `fDSd9kqwYW0`, `UfuiWFVvz2E` | `UfuiWFVvz2E` states no hook size at all — its only hook content is "always circle hooks for the bluefin, recommending J hooks for the yellowtail" — so that cite does not carry the claim. | species/yellowtail.md | a troll strike means more fish under the boat — from any boat; a generic 1.5–2 oz jig is the standing follow-up cast | contradicted-by-source | `jo8lMdFZ1bk` | the drill and the schooling rationale verify ("troll rod goes off, one guy grabs the other troll rod… the third guy toss a stick bait, Colt Sniper, surface iron"), but the source scopes it explicitly to a three-man panga — "it's three people, a panga, down there" — so "from any boat" is an extrapolation. | species/yellowtail.md | cheater troll as dead time on a live-bait sportboat between stops; a rail-wide version would be a mess | contradicted-by-source | `NXvqLUZ6qp8` | the window is not between stops — it is DURING a troll hookup while the rail queues for bait: "they going to hook up at the back of the boat and they're reeling in the trolley ride… everybody else is standing by the bait tank waiting to give bait so I will go over to the side… and every once in a while I'll catch another yellowtail". | species/yellowtail.md | California / CDFW — Farnsworth Bank (Catalina) marine closure: only yellowtail and bonito may be kept | contradicted-by-source | `5p6gu14ZC4w` | the kept-species rule is verbatim — "you can only keep yellowtail and Bonita, everything else needs to go back, that's in the uh Marine closure in there, so you can't keep bass or Rockfish or anything" — but the closure is never named Farnsworth: the auto-caption reads "if I were going I'd look at the at the foreign First Bank" inside the Catalina segment. "Farnsworth Bank" is a reconstruction of garbled ASR, and no jurisdiction, agency (CDFW) or MPA designation is stated on camera. ext-verify |
| species/yellowtail.md | one technique per layer, and they are not interchangeable | contradicted-by-source | `HTowqnwAMeA` | every layer mapping verifies (flyline/slow-troll on top, trolled hard baits mid-column as search, "Yo-yo jigs cover the part of the column that surface irons struggle with and that's that mid to lower part", dropper loop on the bottom for inactive fish). The "not interchangeable" framing does not: the same source says "you can't really be glued to a certain technique or style of fishing" and "you can use the same stuff that you use fly lining" for the 20–30 ft divers. | species/yellowtail.md | a dull gaff cost two shots on a big resident yellowtail | contradicted-by-source | `RPSRH0jwyw4` | the dull-gaff and tail-rope details verify ("the tip's not sharp", "you need to sharpen a gaff", "you want me to tail rope for you"), but the species does not: immediately before the gaffing the crew call it "a possible halibut, is not fighting like a Yellow Tail", and the transcript never confirms the gaffed fish was a yellowtail. | species/yellowtail.md | release smaller-grade fish and stop keeping at the boat's allotment — kept fish pile up in the freezer | contradicted-by-source | `QSmE3mdEL28`, `u0scEBby7nA` | `QSmE3mdEL28` carries it closely ("you don't need to catch and keep everything right out of the gate because they're just going to be sitting in the freezer… if they're small then you let them go"), though it names no allotment. `u0scEBby7nA` does not: there the ceiling is an air-freight weight allowance ("400 kilos amongst those 11, which brings us down to 36 kilos per person, including luggage and fish"), the releases shown are limit-driven rather than size-driven, and the freezer is the lodge's holding room for transport, not a surplus rationale |
| species/yellowtail.md | almost all of a yellowtail's blood pumps through the throat artery within minutes, so cut it on deck and hang the fish head-down ~5 minutes before icing | single-source | `Klfb433I3Uk` | the procedure is verbatim — "cut this artery down here because almost all the fish's blood goes through this artery within a matter of minutes… in a bucket upside down like that… five minutes or so… then put on the ice". | techniques/cheater-troll.md | the side-rod rig for working the slide is a swimbait/jig | contradicted-by-source | `NXvqLUZ6qp8` | the transcript names a specific 2 oz leadhead for this use case, used to get the lure down before the next bait/trolling rotation, immediately after the "stick way out on the side" line — the note generalizes it to "swimbait/jig." The caption track is heavily ASR-garbled at this timestamp (~00:10:20, "down in the fraud let head I only use the two out"), but "two out" reading as "two oz" matches this same channel's ASR rendering of "ounce" elsewhere in the transcript (e.g. "3/8 and 1/2 [oz]... down to 1/4 [oz]"). The same generalization is already flagged under species/yellowtail.md's cheater-troll paragraph, which additionally flags the "between stops" framing (not an issue in this note, which already says "hookup/chum stop") |
| techniques/clearing-a-backlash.md | thumb pressure is what caused the jam (the original backlash), and cinches it tighter if applied during recovery | contradicted-by-source | `Vl0BuetK2D0` | the 39-second clip is a bare demonstration — "I'm going to put my reel into gear... back the drag almost completely off... I'm not going to use my thumb at all. I'm just going to slowly pull this line out" — it shows the caster withholding the thumb during recovery but never states or implies WHY, and never addresses what caused the original backlash. The note's causal explanation is an added mechanism, not sourced content; it is also debatable on the physics (classic backlash/overrun is usually attributed to too little thumb control on the cast, not too much). The same unsupported claim is restated verbatim in the note's Common failures section, unflagged there |

Reads as a miscited video ID rather than a content error — Cameron to confirm and retag the cite |
| techniques/deep-drop-swordfishing.md | Duane Diego Mellor's heavier 15 lb lead and a long 150 ft wind-on backs off fast, two or three clicks, down to about 10 lb at the most pulling on the fish | contradicted-by-source | `yMiBtZ7k8-w` | the 15 lb lead / 150 ft wind-on / "twice as much line as you guys have out" numbers belong to Rush Maltz's Gulf Stream (Florida Keys) rig, not to Duane Diego Mellor (SoCal) — Duane states a 7–10.5 lb lead and a 25 ft wind-on earlier in the same video. The speaker with the 15 lb lead also states the opposite of "backs off ... down to about 10 lb": "I can't back off that much because ... I already have a 15 pound lead on there." Wrong-speaker misattribution plus an inverted conditional |
| techniques/deep-drop-swordfishing.md | 80 lb braid main line, some crews drop to 65 lb in hard current (SoCal rigging build) | contradicted-by-source | `yMiBtZ7k8-w` | the 65 lb option is Rush Maltz's (Gulf Stream/East-coast) stated practice ("both I'm using 80 and 65 ... if the current's running really hard I will drop down to that 65"); Duane Diego Mellor, the only SoCal captain in this source, states only 80 lb braid and never mentions 65 lb |
| techniques/deep-drop-swordfishing.md | East-coast contrast: long leaders run out to 60–100 ft instead of SoCal's short-leader system | contradicted-by-source | `yMiBtZ7k8-w` | Rush Maltz's own confirmed rig, tied to the same speaker turn as his already-flagged 15 lb lead, is a 150 ft wind-on with a short 6–7 ft main leader ("we got 150 foot wind on and then ... our six and seven foot main leaders ... twice as much line as you guys have out") — comparable to SoCal's length, not long. The 60 ft/"a buddy ... gone to a hundred foot leader" passage sits in an unlabeled turn right after Duane's confirmed 35 ft-total SoCal answer (25 ft wind-on + 10–15 ft leader) and cannot be confidently pinned to either captain from the unlabeled auto-captions; either way, the corpus's one identified East-coast captain describes a short leader for himself, undercutting the "short SoCal vs. long East-coast" framing the contrast asserts |
